to a psychiatrist to evaluate whether there was an underlying condition contributing to his long-standing but now work-impairing restlessness.\n- Chief Complaint: “I just can’t sit still in these meetings or trainings anymore—I feel like I’ve got to get up and move even when everyone else is fine staying put.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): In those long safety meetings I last maybe ten minutes before my legs start jumping and I feel this buzzing under my skin, like I’m going to crawl out of my chair if I don’t stand up and walk the halls for a minute, even though I know it annoys my boss when I keep getting up. \n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): Even when I’m just at home watching a game with my wife or sitting in church, I end up whispering comments, tapping my feet, or fiddling with something, because just sitting there quietly without making some kind of noise or movement drives me nuts. \n- S014 (Constant_Activity): My kids joke that I’m like a wind‑up toy because from the time I get up I’m pacing, straightening things, checking doors, or finding some chore to do, and if I try to just sit on the couch on my day off I last a couple minutes before I’m up again doing something. \n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In the staff briefings, I keep cutting people off and answering questions before my supervisor even finishes explaining what he’s asking, and then I realize I misunderstood because I jumped in too fast. \n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): Whether it’s waiting my turn to talk in a meeting, standing in line at the supply office, or even playing bingo with my wife, I get impatient, shift around, sigh, and sometimes just jump in out of turn because I feel like I can’t stand the waiting. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_age_12_years: My mom still tells stories about how in grade school the teachers were always calling her because I couldn’t stay in my seat, was always blurting out answers, and got sent out of class for wandering around the room when everyone else was supposed to be sitting quietly.\n- functional_impairment: Since taking on the supervisor role, my restlessness and jumping in out of turn have led to me missing parts of trainings, messing up the new scheduling system, and getting written up after building managers complained about missed inspection times, and it’s also caused arguments with my wife when I can’t sit through church or bingo like I promised.\n- duration: These problems have really caused trouble on the job for about a It happens at home too—my wife and kids joke that I'm like a wind-up toy because I can't even sit through church, bingo, or a game on TV without pacing, tapping, or fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these restlessness or hyperactivity symptoms already cause problems for you at school or at home, and have they continued consistently since then?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely go back to my childhood; my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers called home because I couldn't stay in my seat, kept blurting out answers, and got sent out of class for wandering. While I've always been active and talkative, it never caused this much trouble until my promotion a year and a half ago. Since then, having to sit through those long meetings has made it impossible to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do your restlessness and difficulty sitting still cause problems in more than one area of life, such as at work, at home, or in social situations, and not just during stressful meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely affects more than just work. It causes arguments with my wife because I can't sit still through church or bingo nights, and I can't even watch a game on the couch without constantly moving or making noise. On top of that, it's led to me messing up our new scheduling system at work and getting written up.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors continue steadily from childhood into adulthood, or did they start only after your job promotion?\n\nPatient: I've actually been this way my entire life, always known as the guy who is constantly on the move. It only became a major problem about a year and a half ago when my promotion forced me into a desk-bound, meeting-heavy role. Before that, my active job let me work around it, but now I'm expected to sit still for hours.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had frequent impulsive or rule-breaking behaviors since childhood, such as interrupting others, acting without thinking, getting into fights, lying, stealing, or being suspended from school?\n\nPatient: I definitely had trouble with interrupting and blurting out answers in school, which got me sent out of the classroom quite a bit. But I never got into fights, lied, or stole—it was just me being unable to keep quiet or stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, such as making careless mistakes, not finishing tasks, or being easily distracted, or is it mainly the restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely the restlessness and impulsivity rather than being easily distracted. The real issue is that I just can't sit still, and my impatience makes me rush through things or jump in too fast before I've even finished listening."

Any mistakes I make, like with our new scheduling system, are more about me rushing through it and being impatient because I'm just itching to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this same restlessness or need to move around since childhood or in more than one setting, like at home as well as at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and kept wandering around. On top of that, it's led to me messing up our new scheduling system at work and getting written up.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors continue steadily from childhood into adulthood, or did they start only after your job promotion?\n\nPatient: I've actually been this way my entire life, always known as the guy who is constantly on the move. Before that, my active job let me work around it, but now I'm expected to sit still for hours.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had frequent impulsive or rule-breaking behaviors since childhood, such as interrupting others, acting without thinking, getting into fights, lying, stealing, or being suspended from school?\n\nPatient: I definitely had trouble with interrupting and blurting out answers in school, which got me sent out of the classroom quite a bit. But I never got into fights, lied, or stole—it was just me being unable to keep quiet or stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, such as making careless mistakes, not finishing tasks, or being easily distracted, or is it mainly the restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely the restlessness and impulsivity rather than being easily distracted. I also get extremely impatient waiting my turn—whether in meetings, standing in lines, or playing bingo with my wife—and end up jumping in out of turn because I just can't stand the wait."

The real issue is that I just can't sit still, and my impatience makes me rush through things or jump in too fast before I've even finished listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often act before thinking, such as interrupting others, blurting out answers, or making impulsive decisions that cause problems? Or is it mainly the restlessness and inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely both. In staff briefings, I constantly cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes speaking, which often leads to misunderstandings. I also get extremely impatient waiting my turn—whether in meetings, standing in lines, or playing bingo with my wife—and end up jumping in out of turn because I just can't stand the wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had clear problems with impulsivity in daily life, such as interrupting people, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or is it mostly just being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely not just about sitting still; the impulsivity is a major struggle for me too. In our staff briefings, I frequently cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes his question. I also have a really hard time waiting my turn in lines, meetings, or even playing bingo with my wife, which makes me impatient and causes me to jump in out of turn."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I just can’t sit still in these meetings or trainings anymore—I feel like I’ve got to get up and move even when everyone else is fine staying put. It's gotten to the point where it's causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling unable to sit still, do you also often make careless mistakes, have trouble focusing or finishing tasks, or lose things because you’re distracted?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I don't really have trouble with losing things or getting easily distracted by other stuff. Any mistakes I make, like with our new scheduling system, are more about me rushing through it and being impatient because I'm just itching to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this same restlessness or need to move around since childhood or in more than one setting, like at home as well as at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and kept wandering around. It happens at home too—my wife and kids joke that I'm like a wind-up toy because I can't even sit through church, bingo, or a game on TV without pacing, tapping, or fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these restlessness or hyperactivity symptoms already cause problems for you at school or at home, and have they continued consistently since then?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely go back to my childhood; my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers called home because I couldn't stay in my seat, kept blurting out answers, and got sent out of class for wandering. While I've always been active and talkative, it never caused this much trouble until my promotion a year and a half ago. Since then, having to sit through those long meetings has made it impossible to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do your restlessness and difficulty sitting still cause problems in more than one area of life, such as at work, at home, or in social situations, and not just during stressful meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely affects more than just work. It causes arguments with my wife because I can't sit still through church or bingo nights, and I can't even watch a game on the couch without constantly moving or making noise. On top of that, it's led to me messing up our new scheduling system at work and getting written up.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors continue steadily from childhood into adulthood, or did they start only after your job promotion?\n\nPatient: I've actually been this way my entire life, always known as the guy who is constantly on the move. It only became a major problem about a year and a half ago when my promotion forced me into a desk-bound, meeting-heavy role. Before that, my active job let me work around it, but now I'm expected to sit still for hours.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had frequent impulsive or rule-breaking behaviors since childhood, such as interrupting others, acting without thinking, getting into fights, lying, stealing, or being suspended from school?\n\nPatient: I definitely had trouble with interrupting and blurting out answers in school, which got me sent out of the classroom quite a bit. But I never got into fights, lied, or stole—it was just me being unable to keep quiet or stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, such as making careless mistakes, not finishing tasks, or being easily distracted, or is it mainly the restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely the restlessness and impulsivity rather than being easily distracted. The real issue is that I just can't sit still, and my impatience makes me rush through things or jump in too fast before I've even finished listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often act before thinking, such as interrupting others, blurting out answers, or making impulsive decisions that cause problems? Or is it mainly the restlessness and inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely both. In staff briefings, I constantly cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes speaking, which often leads to misunderstandings. I also get extremely impatient waiting my turn—whether in meetings, standing in lines, or playing bingo with my wife—and end up jumping in out of turn because I just can't stand the wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had clear problems with impulsivity in daily life, such as interrupting people, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or is it mostly just being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely not just about sitting still; the impulsivity is a major struggle for me too. In our staff briefings, I frequently cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes his question. I also have a really hard time waiting my turn in lines, meetings, or even playing bingo with my wife, which makes me impatient and causes me to jump in out of turn.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you also have clear impulsive behavior problems at school or home, like interrupting, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or was it mainly being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both even back then. My mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and would wander around the room. At the same time, I was always blurting out answers instead of waiting my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, like being easily distracted, losing things, or not finishing tasks, or is it mainly hyperactivity and impulsivity?"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I just can’t sit still in these meetings or trainings anymore—I feel like I’ve got to get up and move even when everyone else is fine staying put. It's gotten to the point where it's causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling unable to sit still, do you also often make careless mistakes, have trouble focusing or finishing tasks, or lose things because you’re distracted?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I don't really have trouble with losing things or getting easily distracted by other stuff. Any mistakes I make, like with our new scheduling system, are more about me rushing through it and being impatient because I'm just itching to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this same restlessness or need to move around since childhood or in more than one setting, like at home as well as at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and kept wandering around. It happens at home too—my wife and kids joke that I'm like a wind-up toy because I can't even sit through church, bingo, or a game on TV without pacing, tapping, or fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these restlessness or hyperactivity symptoms already cause problems for you at school or at home, and have they continued consistently since then?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely go back to my childhood; my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers called home because I couldn't stay in my seat, kept blurting out answers, and got sent out of class for wandering. While I've always been active and talkative, it never caused this much trouble until my promotion a year and a half ago. Since then, having to sit through those long meetings has made it impossible to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do your restlessness and difficulty sitting still cause problems in more than one area of life, such as at work, at home, or in social situations, and not just during stressful meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely affects more than just work. It causes arguments with my wife because I can't sit still through church or bingo nights, and I can't even watch a game on the couch without constantly moving or making noise. On top of that, it's led to me messing up our new scheduling system at work and getting written up.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors continue steadily from childhood into adulthood, or did they start only after your job promotion?\n\nPatient: I've actually been this way my entire life, always known as the guy who is constantly on the move. It only became a major problem about a year and a half ago when my promotion forced me into a desk-bound, meeting-heavy role. Before that, my active job let me work around it, but now I'm expected to sit still for hours.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had frequent impulsive or rule-breaking behaviors since childhood, such as interrupting others, acting without thinking, getting into fights, lying, stealing, or being suspended from school?\n\nPatient: I definitely had trouble with interrupting and blurting out answers in school, which got me sent out of the classroom quite a bit. But I never got into fights, lied, or stole—it was just me being unable to keep quiet or stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, such as making careless mistakes, not finishing tasks, or being easily distracted, or is it mainly the restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely the restlessness and impulsivity rather than being easily distracted. The real issue is that I just can't sit still, and my impatience makes me rush through things or jump in too fast before I've even finished listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often act before thinking, such as interrupting others, blurting out answers, or making impulsive decisions that cause problems? Or is it mainly the restlessness and inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely both. In staff briefings, I constantly cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes speaking, which often leads to misunderstandings. I also get extremely impatient waiting my turn—whether in meetings, standing in lines, or playing bingo with my wife—and end up jumping in out of turn because I just can't stand the wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had clear problems with impulsivity in daily life, such as interrupting people, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or is it mostly just being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely not just about sitting still; the impulsivity is a major struggle for me too. In our staff briefings, I frequently cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes his question. I also have a really hard time waiting my turn in lines, meetings, or even playing bingo with my wife, which makes me impatient and causes me to jump in out of turn.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you also have clear impulsive behavior problems at school or home, like interrupting, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or was it mainly being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both even back then. My mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and would wander around the room. At the same time, I was always blurting out answers instead of waiting my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, like being easily distracted, losing things, or not finishing tasks, or is it mainly hyperactivity and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It's almost entirely the hyperactivity and impulsivity. I don't really have trouble with losing things or being easily distracted. Instead, my biggest issues are this intense physical restlessness where I just can't sit still, my tendency to blurt out answers, and my extreme impatience.\n\nDoctor: It sounds like your main problems are hyperactivity and impulsivity rather than inattention. Have you ever had a long-term pattern of being easily distracted, losing things, or not finishing tasks because your mind wanders, even outside of stressful situations?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't really noticed those kinds of problems with distraction or losing things. My main struggle has always been the physical restlessness, needing to move, and blurting things out. It's the constant urge to be active that gets in my way, rather than my mind wandering.

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I just can’t sit still in these meetings or trainings anymore—I feel like I’ve got to get up and move even when everyone else is fine staying put. It's gotten to the point where it's causing some real issues for me at work.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling unable to sit still, do you also often make careless mistakes, have trouble focusing or finishing tasks, or lose things because you’re distracted?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I don't really have trouble with losing things or getting easily distracted by other stuff. Any mistakes I make, like with our new scheduling system, are more about me rushing through it and being impatient because I'm just itching to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this same restlessness or need to move around since childhood or in more than one setting, like at home as well as at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and kept wandering around. It happens at home too—my wife and kids joke that I'm like a wind-up toy because I can't even sit through church, bingo, or a game on TV without pacing, tapping, or fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these restlessness or hyperactivity symptoms already cause problems for you at school or at home, and have they continued consistently since then?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely go back to my childhood; my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers called home because I couldn't stay in my seat, kept blurting out answers, and got sent out of class for wandering. While I've always been active and talkative, it never caused this much trouble until my promotion a year and a half ago. Since then, having to sit through those long meetings has made it impossible to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do your restlessness and difficulty sitting still cause problems in more than one area of life, such as at work, at home, or in social situations, and not just during stressful meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely affects more than just work. It causes arguments with my wife because I can't sit still through church or bingo nights, and I can't even watch a game on the couch without constantly moving or making noise. On top of that, it's led to me messing up our new scheduling system at work and getting written up.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors continue steadily from childhood into adulthood, or did they start only after your job promotion?\n\nPatient: I've actually been this way my entire life, always known as the guy who is constantly on the move. It only became a major problem about a year and a half ago when my promotion forced me into a desk-bound, meeting-heavy role. Before that, my active job let me work around it, but now I'm expected to sit still for hours.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had frequent impulsive or rule-breaking behaviors since childhood, such as interrupting others, acting without thinking, getting into fights, lying, stealing, or being suspended from school?\n\nPatient: I definitely had trouble with interrupting and blurting out answers in school, which got me sent out of the classroom quite a bit. But I never got into fights, lied, or stole—it was just me being unable to keep quiet or stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, such as making careless mistakes, not finishing tasks, or being easily distracted, or is it mainly the restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely the restlessness and impulsivity rather than being easily distracted. The real issue is that I just can't sit still, and my impatience makes me rush through things or jump in too fast before I've even finished listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often act before thinking, such as interrupting others, blurting out answers, or making impulsive decisions that cause problems? Or is it mainly the restlessness and inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely both. In staff briefings, I constantly cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes speaking, which often leads to misunderstandings. I also get extremely impatient waiting my turn—whether in meetings, standing in lines, or playing bingo with my wife—and end up jumping in out of turn because I just can't stand the wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had clear problems with impulsivity in daily life, such as interrupting people, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or is it mostly just being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely not just about sitting still; the impulsivity is a major struggle for me too. In our staff briefings, I frequently cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes his question. I also have a really hard time waiting my turn in lines, meetings, or even playing bingo with my wife, which makes me impatient and causes me to jump in out of turn.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you also have clear impulsive behavior problems at school or home, like interrupting, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or was it mainly being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both even back then. My mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and would wander around the room. At the same time, I was always blurting out answers instead of waiting my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, like being easily distracted, losing things, or not finishing tasks, or is it mainly hyperactivity and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It's almost entirely the hyperactivity and impulsivity. I don't really have trouble with losing things or being easily distracted. Instead, my biggest issues are this intense physical restlessness where I just can't sit still, my tendency to blurt out answers, and my extreme impatience.\n\nDoctor: It sounds like your main problems are hyperactivity and impulsivity rather than inattention. Have you ever had a long-term pattern of being easily distracted, losing things, or not finishing tasks because your mind wanders, even outside of stressful situations?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed those kinds of problems with distraction or losing things. My main struggle has always been the physical restlessness, needing to move, and blurting things out. It's the constant urge to be active that gets in my way, rather than my mind wandering.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors clearly start before age 12 and continue in more than one setting, like at home, school, and work?"
  }

It definitely affects me in multiple settings today too, from being unable to sit still on my days off at home to struggling through church and bingo nights with my wife.

In our staff briefings, I frequently cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes his question. I also have a really hard time waiting my turn in lines, meetings, or even playing bingo with my wife, which makes me impatient and causes me to jump in out of turn.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you also have clear impulsive behavior problems at school or home, like interrupting, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or was it mainly being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both even back then. My mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and would wander around the room. At the same time, I was always blurting out answers instead of waiting my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, like being easily distracted, losing things, or not finishing tasks, or is it mainly hyperactivity and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It's almost entirely the hyperactivity and impulsivity. I don't really have trouble with losing things or being easily distracted. Instead, my biggest issues are this intense physical restlessness where I just can't sit still, my tendency to blurt out answers, and my extreme impatience.\n\nDoctor: It sounds like your main problems are hyperactivity and impulsivity rather than inattention. Have you ever had a long-term pattern of being easily distracted, losing things, or not finishing tasks because your mind wanders, even outside of stressful situations?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed those kinds of problems with distraction or losing things. My main struggle has always been the physical restlessness, needing to move, and blurting things out. It's the constant urge to be active that gets in my way, rather than my mind wandering.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors clearly start before age 12 and continue in more than one setting, like at home, school, and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, they absolutely started before age 12; my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers called her because I couldn't stay in my seat, wandered the classroom, and blurted out answers. It definitely affects me in multiple settings today too, from being unable to sit still on my days off at home to struggling through church and bingo nights with my wife."

Any mistakes I make, like with our new scheduling system, are more about me rushing through it and being impatient because I'm just itching to get up and move.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this same restlessness or need to move around since childhood or in more than one setting, like at home as well as at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and kept wandering around. It happens at home too—my wife and kids joke that I'm like a wind-up toy because I can't even sit through church, bingo, or a game on TV without pacing, tapping, or fidgeting.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these restlessness or hyperactivity symptoms already cause problems for you at school or at home, and have they continued consistently since then?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely go back to my childhood; my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers called home because I couldn't stay in my seat, kept blurting out answers, and got sent out of class for wandering. While I've always been active and talkative, it never caused this much trouble until my promotion a year and a half ago. Since then, having to sit through those long meetings has made it impossible to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do your restlessness and difficulty sitting still cause problems in more than one area of life, such as at work, at home, or in social situations, and not just during stressful meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely affects more than just work. It causes arguments with my wife because I can't sit still through church or bingo nights, and I can't even watch a game on the couch without constantly moving or making noise. On top of that, it's led to me messing up our new scheduling system at work and getting written up.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors continue steadily from childhood into adulthood, or did they start only after your job promotion?\n\nPatient: I've actually been this way my entire life, always known as the guy who is constantly on the move. It only became a major problem about a year and a half ago when my promotion forced me into a desk-bound, meeting-heavy role. Before that, my active job let me work around it, but now I'm expected to sit still for hours.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had frequent impulsive or rule-breaking behaviors since childhood, such as interrupting others, acting without thinking, getting into fights, lying, stealing, or being suspended from school?\n\nPatient: I definitely had trouble with interrupting and blurting out answers in school, which got me sent out of the classroom quite a bit. But I never got into fights, lied, or stole—it was just me being unable to keep quiet or stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, such as making careless mistakes, not finishing tasks, or being easily distracted, or is it mainly the restlessness and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely the restlessness and impulsivity rather than being easily distracted. The real issue is that I just can't sit still, and my impatience makes me rush through things or jump in too fast before I've even finished listening.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often act before thinking, such as interrupting others, blurting out answers, or making impulsive decisions that cause problems? Or is it mainly the restlessness and inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely both. In staff briefings, I constantly cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes speaking, which often leads to misunderstandings. I also get extremely impatient waiting my turn—whether in meetings, standing in lines, or playing bingo with my wife—and end up jumping in out of turn because I just can't stand the wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had clear problems with impulsivity in daily life, such as interrupting people, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or is it mostly just being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It is definitely not just about sitting still; the impulsivity is a major struggle for me too. In our staff briefings, I frequently cut people off and blurt out answers before my supervisor even finishes his question. I also have a really hard time waiting my turn in lines, meetings, or even playing bingo with my wife, which makes me impatient and causes me to jump in out of turn.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you also have clear impulsive behavior problems at school or home, like interrupting, blurting out answers, or acting without thinking, or was it mainly being unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: It was definitely a mix of both even back then. My mom still talks about how my grade school teachers would call her because I couldn't stay in my seat and would wander around the room. At the same time, I was always blurting out answers instead of waiting my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with inattention, like being easily distracted, losing things, or not finishing tasks, or is it mainly hyperactivity and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It's almost entirely the hyperactivity and impulsivity. I don't really have trouble with losing things or being easily distracted. Instead, my biggest issues are this intense physical restlessness where I just can't sit still, my tendency to blurt out answers, and my extreme impatience.\n\nDoctor: It sounds like your main problems are hyperactivity and impulsivity rather than inattention. Have you ever had a long-term pattern of being easily distracted, losing things, or not finishing tasks because your mind wanders, even outside of stressful situations?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed those kinds of problems with distraction or losing things. My main struggle has always been the physical restlessness, needing to move, and blurting things out. It's the constant urge to be active that gets in my way, rather than my mind wandering.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors clearly start before age 12 and continue in more than one setting, like at home, school, and work?\n\nPatient: Yes, they absolutely started before age 12; my mom still talks about how my grade school teachers called her because I couldn't stay in my seat, wandered the classroom, and blurted out answers. It definitely affects me in multiple settings today too, from being unable to sit still on my days off at home to struggling through church and bingo nights with my wife.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms continue without a long break from childhood into adulthood, or were there periods when they mostly went away for years at a time?\n\nPatient: They've been continuous since childhood, and I've always been known as a highly active, talkative guy who couldn't sit still. It never really went away, but it only became a major problem recently because my previous jobs allowed me to stay on the move all day. Now, this promotion forces me to sit in meetings and offices, which has made it impossible to manage."

Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
